Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, it's nice to meet you too. So, um, the main reason I'm here... well, it's my job. I work on a production line, and it's a pretty strict environment, you know? Like, you have your specific spot and you're supposed to just be there. But I keep leaving my station. I just... I wander off, or like, when we have our shift meetings and everyone is sitting on those metal benches and I'm supposed to stay put, I just keep popping up. 

I really can't help it. It's like my legs just decide to move before my brain even catches up. I don't even realize I'm standing up until I'm already halfway across the floor looking for some tape or just stretching or whatever. Anyway, my supervisor, he's getting really mad about it. He pulled me into the office the other day and told me it's actually becoming a major safety problem. He said I just take off before I even think about it, which, I mean, he's right, but it's not like I'm trying to be dangerous or ignore the rules! I just literally cannot stay glued to one spot for that long, it drives me crazy. 

And since I didn't finish high school, I really, really need to keep this factory job. The pay is actually decent, so Dave—that's my supervisor—basically said I need to get this fixed because you can't just be darting around heavy machinery without looking. So yeah, that's what brought me in. I just need to figure out how to stay in my seat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, wow, yeah. Like, absolutely. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my mom and I were just talking about this the other day when I told her I was coming to see you. Even back in elementary school, like, first or second grade, I was totally that kid. You know, the one who just could not stay in her seat to save her life? 

I remember my second-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she had this big curly hair and always wore these bright floral dresses—she used to get so frustrated with me. We had these little assigned desks with our names taped on them, right? And I was supposed to be at "Desk 4," but I was never actually *at* Desk 4. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or going to look at the class pet—we had a hamster named Barnaby—or just wandering over to see what the kid three rows down was drawing. 

And it wasn't just the wandering, honestly. I was always talking over the teacher, too. Like, she’d be in the middle of explaining a math problem on the chalkboard, and I’d just blurt out whatever popped into my head. I didn't even raise my hand half the time. It was like the thought would hit my brain and just shoot straight out of my mouth before I could stop it. My mom has this whole box in her closet full of notes that got sent home from my teachers. Just stacks of them. "She won't stay seated," "She keeps blurting things out," "She's wandering around the room during quiet reading time." It was a whole thing. So yeah, it's definitely not a new problem, it's just that back then I wasn't going to get fired for it, you know? Just put in time-out or miss recess. But it's always been like this. Always.

Oh, absolutely. It's actually a huge issue for me right now. Like, okay, part of my job isn't just standing on the line, right? Sometimes Dave has me do the inventory logs at the end of the week, just to count what's left on the pallets. Or, well, right now they're actually trying to cross-train me a little bit, which is nice of them considering the wandering thing, so I have to read these CNC training pages. 

And I really, really try. I sit down in the breakroom with this giant heavy binder—it's this hideous bright yellow color, you can't miss it, it has coffee stains all over the front—and I tell myself, "Okay, you're going to focus, you're going to read this and get it done so Dave sees you're taking it seriously." And for the first, I don't know, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm looking at the words, I'm tracking the lines with my finger. 

But then... it's like my brain just shuts off the intake valve or something? The numbers on the inventory sheet or the words in the training manual, they just start to literally blur together on the page. I'll get to the bottom of a page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. None. Not a single word actually stuck in my head. So then I have to go back to the top and start over, because I didn't take any of it in. And then I'll do it *again*, and maybe I'll get distracted by a forklift driving by outside the window, or I'll just start thinking about what I'm having for lunch or if I left my car unlocked, and then I have to read the exact same paragraph for a third time. 

It takes me forever to get through even one page of that CNC stuff because I just can't keep my brain locked onto it. It's exhausting, honestly, just trying to force myself to look at a piece of paper and actually absorb what it says. Dave thinks I'm just dragging my feet to avoid getting back on the floor, but I'm really not! I just can't keep my focus on it no matter how hard I try.

I just literally cannot stay glued to one spot for that long, it drives me crazy. \n\nAnd since I didn't finish high school, I really, really need to keep this factory job. The pay is actually decent, so Dave—that's my supervisor—basically said I need to get this fixed because you can't just be darting around heavy machinery without looking. So yeah, that's what brought me in. I just need to figure out how to stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who had a hard time staying seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yeah. Like, absolutely. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my mom and I were just talking about this the other day when I told her I was coming to see you. Even back in elementary school, like, first or second grade, I was totally that kid. You know, the one who just could not stay in her seat to save her life? \n\nI remember my second-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she had this big curly hair and always wore these bright floral dresses—she used to get so frustrated with me. We had these little assigned desks with our names taped on them, right? And I was supposed to be at \"Desk 4,\" but I was never actually *at* Desk 4. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or going to look at the class pet—we had a hamster named Barnaby—or just wandering over to see what the kid three rows down was drawing. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the wandering, honestly. I was always talking over the teacher, too. Like, she’d be in the middle of explaining a math problem on the chalkboard, and I’d just blurt out whatever popped into my head. I didn't even raise my hand half the time. It was like the thought would hit my brain and just shoot straight out of my mouth before I could stop it. My mom has this whole box in her closet full of notes that got sent home from my teachers. Just stacks of them. \"She won't stay seated,\" \"She keeps blurting things out,\" \"She's wandering around the room during quiet reading time.\" It was a whole thing. So yeah, it's definitely not a new problem, it's just that back then I wasn't going to get fired for it, you know? Just put in time-out or miss recess. But it's always been like this. Always.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused on what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's actually a huge issue for me right now. Like, okay, part of my job isn't just standing on the line, right? Sometimes Dave has me do the inventory logs at the end of the week, just to count what's left on the pallets. Or, well, right now they're actually trying to cross-train me a little bit, which is nice of them considering the wandering thing, so I have to read these CNC training pages. \n\nAnd I really, really try. I sit down in the breakroom with this giant heavy binder—it's this hideous bright yellow color, you can't miss it, it has coffee stains all over the front—and I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to focus, you're going to read this and get it done so Dave sees you're taking it seriously.\" And for the first, I don't know, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm looking at the words, I'm tracking the lines with my finger. \n\nBut then... it's like my brain just shuts off the intake valve or something? The numbers on the inventory sheet or the words in the training manual, they just start to literally blur together on the page. I'll get to the bottom of a page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. None. Not a single word actually stuck in my head. So then I have to go back to the top and start over, because I didn't take any of it in. And then I'll do it *again*, and maybe I'll get distracted by a forklift driving by outside the window, or I'll just start thinking about what I'm having for lunch or if I left my car unlocked, and then I have to read the exact same paragraph for a third time. \n\nIt takes me forever to get through even one page of that CNC stuff because I just can't keep my brain locked onto it. It's exhausting, honestly, just trying to force myself to look at a piece of paper and actually absorb what it says. Dave thinks I'm just dragging my feet to avoid getting back on the floor, but I'm really not! I just can't keep my focus on it no matter how hard I try.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, oh my gosh, yes. It's... it's not even just a feeling of wanting to move, it's like a physical need, you know? Like, when I'm supposed to just stand there by the line—because my station is right between the labeling machine and the packing conveyor, so I'm literally just supposed to stand in this one little square taped on the floor—it's awful. Or when I'm sitting in those training sessions with Dave in the breakroom, trying to read that yellow binder. 

If I try to just stay still, it feels like... I don't know how to explain it. Like there's this actual physical pressure building up inside my body? Like carbonation in a soda bottle that got shaken up. It starts in my legs, usually, just this tight, fizzy feeling, and it creeps up into my chest, and it just gets stronger and stronger until I feel like I'm going to explode if I don't do something. 

I *have* to move. I have to pace back and forth, or stretch my arms out, or just go find something to do right that exact second. Sometimes I'll just start reorganizing the tape dispensers or walking over to check the trash cans, even if they're empty, just to bleed off some of that pressure. It's like my body is humming on the inside, and standing still is just... it's physically uncomfortable. It's almost painful, honestly, just trying to force myself to stay in one spot when every single nerve in my body is screaming at me to go, go, go.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who had a hard time staying seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yeah. Like, absolutely. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my mom and I were just talking about this the other day when I told her I was coming to see you. Even back in elementary school, like, first or second grade, I was totally that kid. You know, the one who just could not stay in her seat to save her life? \n\nI remember my second-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she had this big curly hair and always wore these bright floral dresses—she used to get so frustrated with me. We had these little assigned desks with our names taped on them, right? And I was supposed to be at \"Desk 4,\" but I was never actually *at* Desk 4. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or going to look at the class pet—we had a hamster named Barnaby—or just wandering over to see what the kid three rows down was drawing. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the wandering, honestly. I was always talking over the teacher, too. Like, she’d be in the middle of explaining a math problem on the chalkboard, and I’d just blurt out whatever popped into my head. I didn't even raise my hand half the time. It was like the thought would hit my brain and just shoot straight out of my mouth before I could stop it. My mom has this whole box in her closet full of notes that got sent home from my teachers. Just stacks of them. \"She won't stay seated,\" \"She keeps blurting things out,\" \"She's wandering around the room during quiet reading time.\" It was a whole thing. So yeah, it's definitely not a new problem, it's just that back then I wasn't going to get fired for it, you know? Just put in time-out or miss recess. But it's always been like this. Always.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused on what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's actually a huge issue for me right now. Like, okay, part of my job isn't just standing on the line, right? Sometimes Dave has me do the inventory logs at the end of the week, just to count what's left on the pallets. Or, well, right now they're actually trying to cross-train me a little bit, which is nice of them considering the wandering thing, so I have to read these CNC training pages. \n\nAnd I really, really try. I sit down in the breakroom with this giant heavy binder—it's this hideous bright yellow color, you can't miss it, it has coffee stains all over the front—and I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to focus, you're going to read this and get it done so Dave sees you're taking it seriously.\" And for the first, I don't know, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm looking at the words, I'm tracking the lines with my finger. \n\nBut then... it's like my brain just shuts off the intake valve or something? The numbers on the inventory sheet or the words in the training manual, they just start to literally blur together on the page. I'll get to the bottom of a page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. None. Not a single word actually stuck in my head. So then I have to go back to the top and start over, because I didn't take any of it in. And then I'll do it *again*, and maybe I'll get distracted by a forklift driving by outside the window, or I'll just start thinking about what I'm having for lunch or if I left my car unlocked, and then I have to read the exact same paragraph for a third time. \n\nIt takes me forever to get through even one page of that CNC stuff because I just can't keep my brain locked onto it. It's exhausting, honestly, just trying to force myself to look at a piece of paper and actually absorb what it says. Dave thinks I'm just dragging my feet to avoid getting back on the floor, but I'm really not! I just can't keep my focus on it no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Besides trouble focusing, do you still often feel restless or driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh my gosh, yes. It's... it's not even just a feeling of wanting to move, it's like a physical need, you know? Like, when I'm supposed to just stand there by the line—because my station is right between the labeling machine and the packing conveyor, so I'm literally just supposed to stand in this one little square taped on the floor—it's awful. Or when I'm sitting in those training sessions with Dave in the breakroom, trying to read that yellow binder. \n\nIf I try to just stay still, it feels like... I don't know how to explain it. Like there's this actual physical pressure building up inside my body? Like carbonation in a soda bottle that got shaken up. It starts in my legs, usually, just this tight, fizzy feeling, and it creeps up into my chest, and it just gets stronger and stronger until I feel like I'm going to explode if I don't do something. \n\nI *have* to move. I have to pace back and forth, or stretch my arms out, or just go find something to do right that exact second. Sometimes I'll just start reorganizing the tape dispensers or walking over to check the trash cans, even if they're empty, just to bleed off some of that pressure. It's like my body is humming on the inside, and standing still is just... it's physically uncomfortable. It's almost painful, honestly, just trying to force myself to stay in one spot when every single nerve in my body is screaming at me to go, go, go.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often lose things you need for work or daily life, like tools, papers, keys, or your phone?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / production_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, actually, that's one thing I don't really do. It's funny because you'd think with how much I'm bouncing around all the time, I'd be leaving a trail of stuff behind me, but I really don't. Like, my phone? My phone is literally always either glued to my hand or it's in my left back pocket. Specifically the left one, because my right pocket is for my chapstick and my gum—usually Extra peppermint, the one in the blue pack, I always buy the multi-packs at the grocery store. If my phone isn't in that exact left pocket, I instantly notice the weight difference, you know? It's like a phantom limb thing, my brain just flags it immediately. 

And for my keys, I have this massive, bright neon pink lanyard. I actually got it at this random gas station off I-95 when my friends and I drove down to Florida a couple of years ago. It's so obnoxiously bright that even if I did set it down somewhere, you could see it from like a mile away. But I don't even really set it down, I just hang it on the exact same little brass hook by my front door the second I walk into my apartment. 

And at work, Dave—my supervisor, the one I mentioned earlier—he used to always get on my case when I first started. He was always telling me I was going to lose my safety cutter or leave the tape gun somewhere across the warehouse just because I'm always walking away

I sit down in the breakroom with this giant heavy binder—it's this hideous bright yellow color, you can't miss it, it has coffee stains all over the front—and I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to focus, you're going to read this and get it done so Dave sees you're taking it seriously.\" And for the first, I don't know, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm looking at the words, I'm tracking the lines with my finger. \n\nBut then... it's like my brain just shuts off the intake valve or something? I'll get to the bottom of a page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. None. Not a single word actually stuck in my head. So then I have to go back to the top and start over, because I didn't take any of it in. No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm pretty good about catching things, honestly. Like, I don't really mess up the labels or anything like that. Oh, yeah, definitely. It's not just a work thing at all. It happens everywhere. Like, at home, I can't just sit and watch a movie. My roommate, Sarah—we’ve lived together since we dropped out in 11th grade, we share this tiny two-bedroom apartment over by the highway—she loves movie nights. She’ll make popcorn, the kind with the extra butter, and she just wants to sit on the couch and watch, like, a two-hour movie. And I just... I can't. I'll sit down for maybe fifteen minutes, and then that fizzy feeling starts again, right in my legs. 

So I’ll get up and say I need water, and then while I’m in the kitchen, I’ll start wiping down the counters, or I’ll decide right then that the fridge needs to be reorganized because the milk is on the wrong shelf. And Sarah will be yelling from the living room, "Are you watching this?" and I'll be like, "Yeah, yeah, I'm listening!" But I'm literally pacing around the kitchen island. 

Or if we go out to eat, like to a restaurant? That’s the worst. Sitting in a booth for an hour waiting for food... I end up bouncing my leg so hard the whole table shakes, and I’m constantly getting up to go to the bathroom, not because I actually need to go, but just because I need an excuse to walk across the restaurant. I’ll go check out the dessert display, or I’ll stand by the hostess stand and look at the menus they have piled up, just doing anything to not be sitting in that booth. It drives Sarah crazy. She’s always telling me to just sit down and relax, but I literally don't know how to do that. It’s like my body doesn't have an "off" switch, no matter where I am.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, it's nice to meet you too. So, um, the main reason I'm here... well, it's my job. I work on a production line, and it's a pretty strict environment, you know? Like, you have your specific spot and you're supposed to just be there. But I keep leaving my station. I just... I wander off, or like, when we have our shift meetings and everyone is sitting on those metal benches and I'm supposed to stay put, I just keep popping up. \n\nI really can't help it. It's like my legs just decide to move before my brain even catches up. I don't even realize I'm standing up until I'm already halfway across the floor looking for some tape or just stretching or whatever. Anyway, my supervisor, he's getting really mad about it. He pulled me into the office the other day and told me it's actually becoming a major safety problem. He said I just take off before I even think about it, which, I mean, he's right, but it's not like I'm trying to be dangerous or ignore the rules! I just literally cannot stay glued to one spot for that long, it drives me crazy. \n\nAnd since I didn't finish high school, I really, really need to keep this factory job. The pay is actually decent, so Dave—that's my supervisor—basically said I need to get this fixed because you can't just be darting around heavy machinery without looking. So yeah, that's what brought me in. I just need to figure out how to stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who had a hard time staying seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yeah. Like, absolutely. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my mom and I were just talking about this the other day when I told her I was coming to see you. Even back in elementary school, like, first or second grade, I was totally that kid. You know, the one who just could not stay in her seat to save her life? \n\nI remember my second-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she had this big curly hair and always wore these bright floral dresses—she used to get so frustrated with me. We had these little assigned desks with our names taped on them, right? And I was supposed to be at \"Desk 4,\" but I was never actually *at* Desk 4. I'd be up sharpening my pencil for the fifth time, or going to look at the class pet—we had a hamster named Barnaby—or just wandering over to see what the kid three rows down was drawing. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the wandering, honestly. I was always talking over the teacher, too. Like, she’d be in the middle of explaining a math problem on the chalkboard, and I’d just blurt out whatever popped into my head. I didn't even raise my hand half the time. It was like the thought would hit my brain and just shoot straight out of my mouth before I could stop it. My mom has this whole box in her closet full of notes that got sent home from my teachers. Just stacks of them. \"She won't stay seated,\" \"She keeps blurting things out,\" \"She's wandering around the room during quiet reading time.\" It was a whole thing. So yeah, it's definitely not a new problem, it's just that back then I wasn't going to get fired for it, you know? Just put in time-out or miss recess. But it's always been like this. Always.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention or staying focused on what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's actually a huge issue for me right now. Like, okay, part of my job isn't just standing on the line, right? Sometimes Dave has me do the inventory logs at the end of the week, just to count what's left on the pallets. Or, well, right now they're actually trying to cross-train me a little bit, which is nice of them considering the wandering thing, so I have to read these CNC training pages. \n\nAnd I really, really try. I sit down in the breakroom with this giant heavy binder—it's this hideous bright yellow color, you can't miss it, it has coffee stains all over the front—and I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to focus, you're going to read this and get it done so Dave sees you're taking it seriously.\" And for the first, I don't know, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm looking at the words, I'm tracking the lines with my finger. \n\nBut then... it's like my brain just shuts off the intake valve or something? The numbers on the inventory sheet or the words in the training manual, they just start to literally blur together on the page. I'll get to the bottom of a page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. None. Not a single word actually stuck in my head. So then I have to go back to the top and start over, because I didn't take any of it in. And then I'll do it *again*, and maybe I'll get distracted by a forklift driving by outside the window, or I'll just start thinking about what I'm having for lunch or if I left my car unlocked, and then I have to read the exact same paragraph for a third time. \n\nIt takes me forever to get through even one page of that CNC stuff because I just can't keep my brain locked onto it. It's exhausting, honestly, just trying to force myself to look at a piece of paper and actually absorb what it says. Dave thinks I'm just dragging my feet to avoid getting back on the floor, but I'm really not! I just can't keep my focus on it no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Besides trouble focusing, do you still often feel restless or driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh my gosh, yes. It's... it's not even just a feeling of wanting to move, it's like a physical need, you know? Like, when I'm supposed to just stand there by the line—because my station is right between the labeling machine and the packing conveyor, so I'm literally just supposed to stand in this one little square taped on the floor—it's awful. Or when I'm sitting in those training sessions with Dave in the breakroom, trying to read that yellow binder. \n\nIf I try to just stay still, it feels like... I don't know how to explain it. Like there's this actual physical pressure building up inside my body? Like carbonation in a soda bottle that got shaken up. It starts in my legs, usually, just this tight, fizzy feeling, and it creeps up into my chest, and it just gets stronger and stronger until I feel like I'm going to explode if I don't do something. \n\nI *have* to move. I have to pace back and forth, or stretch my arms out, or just go find something to do right that exact second. Sometimes I'll just start reorganizing the tape dispensers or walking over to check the trash cans, even if they're empty, just to bleed off some of that pressure. It's like my body is humming on the inside, and standing still is just... it's physically uncomfortable. It's almost painful, honestly, just trying to force myself to stay in one spot when every single nerve in my body is screaming at me to go, go, go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for work or daily life, like tools, papers, keys, or your phone?\n\nPatient: No, actually, that's one thing I don't really do. It's funny because you'd think with how much I'm bouncing around all the time, I'd be leaving a trail of stuff behind me, but I really don't. Like, my phone? My phone is literally always either glued to my hand or it's in my left back pocket. Specifically the left one, because my right pocket is for my chapstick and my gum—usually Extra peppermint, the one in the blue pack, I always buy the multi-packs at the grocery store. If my phone isn't in that exact left pocket, I instantly notice the weight difference, you know? It's like a phantom limb thing, my brain just flags it immediately. \n\nAnd for my keys, I have this massive, bright neon pink lanyard. I actually got it at this random gas station off I-95 when my friends and I drove down to Florida a couple of years ago. It's so obnoxiously bright that even if I did set it down somewhere, you could see it from like a mile away. But I don't even really set it down, I just hang it on the exact same little brass hook by my front door the second I walk into my apartment. \n\nAnd at work, Dave—my supervisor, the one I mentioned earlier—he used to always get on my case when I first started. He was always telling me I was going to lose my safety cutter or leave the tape gun somewhere across the warehouse just because I'm always walking away\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes because you miss details?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm pretty good about catching things, honestly. Like, I don't really mess up the labels or anything like that. When I'm at my station, between the labeling machine and the packing conveyor—the one with the square taped on the floor, you know—I'm actually really careful about making sure the right labels go on the right boxes. Dave even said a couple of weeks ago that my accuracy rate on the line is actually one of the highest on the shift, which was nice to hear because sometimes I feel like he's just always waiting for me to screw up since I'm always moving around so much. \n\nAnd even with the inventory logs, the ones I have to do at the end of the week? Even though it's super hard for me to focus on the numbers and they start blurring together like I was saying before, I don't really make mistakes on them. I just have to read them over and over and over again, but once I actually get the numbers down, they're right. I'm not, like, accidentally writing a 5 instead of an 8 or forgetting to count a whole pallet or anything. I just take a really long time to do it. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I make careless mistakes. I'm actually pretty detail-oriented when I'm actually doing the thing, it's just the *staying* there to do the thing that's the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do these problems with getting up and moving around also happen in other situations outside of work, like at home or when you’re with other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. It's not just a work thing at all. It happens everywhere. Like, at home, I can't just sit and watch a movie. My roommate, Sarah—we’ve lived together since we dropped out in 11th grade, we share this tiny two-bedroom apartment over by the highway—she loves movie nights. She’ll make popcorn, the kind with the extra butter, and she just wants to sit on the couch and watch, like, a two-hour movie. And I just... I can't. I'll sit down for maybe fifteen minutes, and then that fizzy feeling starts again, right in my legs. \n\nSo I’ll get up and say I need water, and then while I’m in the kitchen, I’ll start wiping down the counters, or I’ll decide right then that the fridge needs to be reorganized because the milk is on the wrong shelf. And Sarah will be yelling from the living room, \"Are you watching this?\" and I'll be like, \"Yeah, yeah, I'm listening!\" But I'm literally pacing around the kitchen island. \n\nOr if we go out to eat, like to a restaurant? That’s the worst. Sitting in a booth for an hour waiting for food... I end up bouncing my leg so hard the whole table shakes, and I’m constantly getting up to go to the bathroom, not because I actually need to go, but just because I need an excuse to walk across the restaurant. I’ll go check out the dessert display, or I’ll stand by the hostess stand and look at the menus they have piled up, just doing anything to not be sitting in that booth. It drives Sarah crazy. She’s always telling me to just sit down and relax, but I literally don't know how to do that. It’s like my body doesn't have an \"off\" switch, no matter where I am.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's so obnoxiously bright that even if I did set it down somewhere, you could see it from like a mile away. But I don't even really set it down, I just hang it on the exact same little brass hook by my front door the second I walk into my apartment. \n\nAnd at work, Dave—my supervisor, the one I mentioned earlier—he used to always get on my case when I first started. He was always telling me I was going to lose my safety cutter or leave the tape gun somewhere across the warehouse just because I'm always walking away\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes because you miss details?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm pretty good about catching things, honestly. Like, I don't really mess up the labels or anything like that. When I'm at my station, between the labeling machine and the packing conveyor—the one with the square taped on the floor, you know—I'm actually really careful about making sure the right labels go on the right boxes. Dave even said a couple of weeks ago that my accuracy rate on the line is actually one of the highest on the shift, which was nice to hear because sometimes I feel like he's just always waiting for me to screw up since I'm always moving around so much. \n\nAnd even with the inventory logs, the ones I have to do at the end of the week? Even though it's super hard for me to focus on the numbers and they start blurring together like I was saying before, I don't really make mistakes on them. I just have to read them over and over and over again, but once I actually get the numbers down, they're right. I'm not, like, accidentally writing a 5 instead of an 8 or forgetting to count a whole pallet or anything. I just take a really long time to do it. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I make careless mistakes. I'm actually pretty detail-oriented when I'm actually doing the thing, it's just the *staying* there to do the thing that's the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do these problems with getting up and moving around also happen in other situations outside of work, like at home or when you’re with other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. It's not just a work thing at all. It happens everywhere. Like, at home, I can't just sit and watch a movie. My roommate, Sarah—we’ve lived together since we dropped out in 11th grade, we share this tiny two-bedroom apartment over by the highway—she loves movie nights. She’ll make popcorn, the kind with the extra butter, and she just wants to sit on the couch and watch, like, a two-hour movie. And I just... I can't. I'll sit down for maybe fifteen minutes, and then that fizzy feeling starts again, right in my legs. \n\nSo I’ll get up and say I need water, and then while I’m in the kitchen, I’ll start wiping down the counters, or I’ll decide right then that the fridge needs to be reorganized because the milk is on the wrong shelf. And Sarah will be yelling from the living room, \"Are you watching this?\" and I'll be like, \"Yeah, yeah, I'm listening!\" But I'm literally pacing around the kitchen island. \n\nOr if we go out to eat, like to a restaurant? That’s the worst. Sitting in a booth for an hour waiting for food... I end up bouncing my leg so hard the whole table shakes, and I’m constantly getting up to go to the bathroom, not because I actually need to go, but just because I need an excuse to walk across the restaurant. I’ll go check out the dessert display, or I’ll stand by the hostess stand and look at the menus they have piled up, just doing anything to not be sitting in that booth. It drives Sarah crazy. She’s always telling me to just sit down and relax, but I literally don't know how to do that. It’s like my body doesn't have an \"off\" switch, no matter where I am.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often interrupt other people before they finish talking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Oh, God, yeah. All the time. It's actually a huge problem, and I feel so bad about it, but I literally can't stop myself. It's like the words are just pushing their way out of my mouth before my brain even realizes what's happening. 

Like at work, right? We have these morning huddles before the shift starts where the lead goes over the goals for the day and any issues from the day before. And he'll be in the middle of asking a question, like, "Does anyone know why the pallet jack was left in aisle four?" and before he even finishes the sentence, I'm already blurting out, "Because the battery died and Maintenance said they'd get it!" And they both just stared at me because they were having a private conversation and I just bulldozed right into it. 

It happens with Sarah, too, my roommate. She'll be telling me a story about her day, and I'll just jump in and finish her sentences, or I'll cut her off to say something that popped into my head that's totally unrelated. She gets so mad. She actually told me last week that she feels like I don't even listen to her, which isn't true! I *am* listening, it's just that my brain is moving so fast that I feel like if I don't say the thing right that exact second, I'm going to explode or I'm going to forget it forever. But yeah, people definitely get irritated with me over it. I can see it on their faces, that look of like, "Can you just let me finish?" And I want to, I really do, but the words just jump out.

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. I sit down in the breakroom with this giant heavy binder—it's this hideous bright yellow color, you can't miss it, it has coffee stains all over the front—and I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to focus, you're going to read this and get it done so Dave sees you're taking it seriously.\" And for the first, I don't know, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm looking at the words, I'm tracking the lines with my finger. \n\nBut then... it's like my brain just shuts off the intake valve or something? The numbers on the inventory sheet or the words in the training manual, they just start to literally blur together on the page. I'll get to the bottom of a page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. None. Not a single word actually stuck in my head. So then I have to go back to the top and start over, because I didn't take any of it in. And then I'll do it *again*, and maybe I'll get distracted by a forklift driving by outside the window, or I'll just start thinking about what I'm having for lunch or if I left my car unlocked, and then I have to read the exact same paragraph for a third time. \n\nIt takes me forever to get through even one page of that CNC stuff because I just can't keep my brain locked onto it. It's exhausting, honestly, just trying to force myself to look at a piece of paper and actually absorb what it says. Dave thinks I'm just dragging my feet to avoid getting back on the floor, but I'm really not! I just can't keep my focus on it no matter how hard I try.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, oh my gosh, yes. It's... it's not even just a feeling of wanting to move, it's like a physical need, you know? Like there's this actual physical pressure building up inside my body? Like carbonation in a soda bottle that got shaken up. It starts in my legs, usually, just this tight, fizzy feeling, and it creeps up into my chest, and it just gets stronger and stronger until I feel like I'm going to explode if I don't do something. \n\nI *have* to move. I have to pace back and forth, or stretch my arms out, or just go find something to do right that exact second. Sometimes I'll just start reorganizing the tape dispensers or walking over to check the trash cans, even if they're empty, just to bleed off some of that pressure. It's like my body is humming on the inside, and standing still is just... it's physically uncomfortable. It's almost painful, honestly, just trying to force myself to stay in one spot when every single nerve in my body is screaming at me to go, go, go.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, actually, that's one thing I don't really do. It's funny because you'd think with how much I'm bouncing around all the time, I'd be leaving a trail of stuff behind me, but I really don't. Like, my phone? My phone is literally always either glued to my hand or it's in my left back pocket. Specifically the left one, because my right pocket is for my chapstick and my gum—usually Extra peppermint, the one in the blue pack, I always buy the multi-packs at the grocery store. If my phone isn't in that exact left pocket, I instantly notice the weight difference, you know? It's like a phantom limb thing, my brain just flags it immediately. \n\nAnd for my keys, I have this massive, bright neon pink lanyard. I actually got it at this random gas station off I-95 when my friends and I drove down to Florida a couple of years ago. It's so obnoxiously bright that even if I did set it down somewhere, you could see it from like a mile away. But I don't even really set it down, I just hang it on the exact same little brass hook by my front door the second I walk into my apartment. \n\nAnd at work, Dave—my supervisor, the one I mentioned earlier—he used to always get on my case when I first started. He was always telling me I was going to lose my safety cutter or leave the tape gun somewhere across the warehouse just because I'm always walking away\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. She'll be telling me a story about her day, and I'll just jump in and finish her sentences, or I'll cut her off to say something that popped into my head that's totally unrelated. She gets so mad. I *am* listening, it's just that my brain is moving so fast that I feel like if I don't say the thing right that exact second, I'm going to explode or I'm going to forget it forever.
